## 2026-03-20T09:21:20+01:00 - Kept watched-slot normalization profiling upgrade

- Objective: quantify how often watched-slot normalization actually happens inside `propagate()`, and how much of it immediately ends in a satisfied skip versus feeding real relocation/unit/conflict work.
- Hypothesis: if normalization is only a small or mostly-satisfied subset of watch traffic, more work on “lazy normalization” would be low priority; if it is frequent and mostly tied to real work, the recent lazy-normalization reject was likely paying extra branching on a path we still need to traverse anyway.
- Files changed: `tools/profile_solver.py`, `tests/test_profile_solver.py`, `agent_log.md`, `experiments.jsonl`, `benchmark_summary.md`, `next_steps.md`
- Tests executed: `python -m unittest discover -s tests -v`; `python tools/profile_solver.py large/test_6.cnf special/hard.cnf medium/test_4.cnf large/test_8.cnf`
- Benchmark results: the profiler now records total watched-slot normalizations, ternary-vs-large normalization counts, and how many normalizations were immediately followed by a satisfied skip. On `large/test_6.cnf`, there were `4,097,859` normalizations (`3,037,779` ternary, `1,060,080` large) out of `13,435,147` watch visits, but only `820,227` of them ended in an immediate satisfied skip, so about `79.98%` of normalizations still flowed into relocation/unit/conflict work. `special/hard.cnf` showed `2,747,520` normalizations with `730,699` immediate satisfied skips (`73.40%` non-skip), `medium/test_4.cnf` showed `591,235` with `142,915` immediate satisfied skips (`75.82%` non-skip), and `large/test_8.cnf` showed `927,548` with `199,008` immediate satisfied skips (`78.54%` non-skip). Ternary clauses dominate normalization counts too: `74.1%` of normalizations on `large/test_6.cnf`, `63.8%` on `special/hard.cnf`, `76.7%` on `medium/test_4.cnf`, and `79.8%` on `large/test_8.cnf`.
- Decision taken: keep the profiler/test upgrade and leave the production solver unchanged. The new counters explain why lazy normalization lost: watched-slot normalization is frequent, but most of it is on clauses that still need real propagation work afterward, so a branchier “maybe normalize later” path is not obviously cheaper.
- Next recommended step: deprioritize more lazy-normalization ideas and keep future propagation work focused on removing larger chunks of ternary traversal/relocation/unit work rather than just trying to dodge the swap itself.

## 2026-03-20T09:30:20+01:00 - Rejected per-visit `len(lits)` caching in `propagate()`

- Objective: test whether caching `len(lits)` once per watched-clause visit can remove enough hot-loop overhead inside `propagate()` to matter on the dense bottlenecks.
- Hypothesis: `cProfile` still shows a lot of `len()` traffic around watched-clause traversal, so a tiny local cache of clause length might help without changing the search path.
- Files changed: `agent_log.md`, `experiments.jsonl`, `benchmark_summary.md`, `next_steps.md`
- Tests executed: out-of-tree same-run five-case timing with retained baseline then temporary `LenCachedSolver`; `python -m unittest discover -s tests -v`; `python satsolver.py small/test_1.cnf /tmp/cycle_sat_check3.txt`; `python tools/checker.py small/test_1.cnf /tmp/cycle_sat_check3.txt`; `python satsolver.py special/tseitin.cnf /tmp/cycle_unsat_check3.txt`; `python tools/checker.py special/tseitin.cnf /tmp/cycle_unsat_check3.txt --bruteforce-var-limit 0`
- Benchmark results: the temporary subclass lost clearly enough that no reverse-order confirmation was warranted. The focused serial five-case slice regressed from `27.5712s` to `29.1215s` (`+5.62%`): `large/test_6.cnf 12.3785s -> 12.7961s`, `special/hard.cnf 8.2641s -> 8.6783s`, `medium/test_4.cnf 1.7486s -> 1.9761s`, `large/test_10.cnf 1.7007s -> 1.7271s`, and `large/test_8.cnf 3.4794s -> 3.9439s`.
- Decision taken: discard the change and keep the retained solver unchanged. This was another pure-overhead loser, not a promising propagation optimization.
- Next recommended step: do not revisit simple per-visit `len(lits)` caching in `propagate()`. Future propagation work still needs to remove a larger chunk of ternary watched-clause traversal work than this local micro-cache did.

## 2026-03-20T09:44:53+01:00 - Kept slice-delete `backtrack()` rewrite

- Objective: cut the remaining `backtrack()` overhead that still shows up clearly in `cProfile`, without changing the solver’s search path.
- Hypothesis: scanning the current trail segment by index and deleting the tail once per decision level should be cheaper than repeated `while len(trail) > start: trail.pop()` cleanup, while preserving the same assignments, reasons, and queue state.
- Files changed: `satsolver.py`, `tests/test_solver_regressions.py`, `out_extended.txt`, `out_cli_extended.txt`, `agent_log.md`, `experiments.jsonl`, `benchmark_summary.md`, `next_steps.md`
- Tests executed: out-of-tree same-run five-case timing with retained baseline then temporary `SliceBacktrackSolver`; reversed-order same-run five-case timing with temporary `SliceBacktrackSolver` then baseline; out-of-tree `cProfile` comparison on `large/test_6.cnf`; `python -m unittest discover -s tests -v`; `python satsolver.py small/test_1.cnf /tmp/cycle_sat_check4.txt`; `python tools/checker.py small/test_1.cnf /tmp/cycle_sat_check4.txt`; `python satsolver.py special/tseitin.cnf /tmp/cycle_unsat_check4.txt`; `python tools/checker.py special/tseitin.cnf /tmp/cycle_unsat_check4.txt --bruteforce-var-limit 0`; `python benchmark_suite.py satsolver out_extended.txt small medium large special satlib_subset satlib_more --bruteforce-var-limit 16 --repeat 2`; `python benchmark_suite.py satsolver out_cli_extended.txt small medium large special satlib_subset satlib_more --bruteforce-var-limit 16 --cli-script satsolver.py --repeat 2`; `python benchmark_suite.py satsolver /tmp/hotspot_repeat.txt large special medium --bruteforce-var-limit 16 --repeat 2`
- Benchmark results: the same-run serial five-case slice improved in both orders: `27.9713s -> 27.0617s` with baseline first, and `28.5337s -> 26.9753s` with the experimental solver first, for a same-day average of `28.2525s -> 27.0185s` (`-4.37%`). On `large/test_6.cnf`, `cProfile` kept the same conflict count (`59,201`) but cut `backtrack()` from `2.189s` to `0.558s`, `pop()` calls from `10,107,994` to `6,745,603`, and total profiled wall time from `30.397s` to `25.975s`. Validation stayed clean with `24` passing unit tests plus SAT/UNSAT checker spot checks. On the full repeat-aware 59-case suite, the in-process path did not set a new best (`26.2118s -> 26.8404s`), but the exact required CLI path improved to a new repeat-aware best at `28.6838s`, down from the previous `28.9681s` latest run and `28.7122s` prior best. The current large/special/medium repeat-aware slice also looked healthy at `24.8900s`, with `large/test_6.cnf 12.1898s`, `special/hard.cnf 7.8706s`, `medium/test_4.cnf 1.7183s`, and `large/test_8.cnf 0.2163s`.
- Decision taken: keep the change. The search-path-preserving hotspot evidence is strong, the exact submission-path benchmark set a new repeat-aware best, and correctness stayed clean. The one caution is that the module-mode repeat-aware 59-case suite did not beat its best historical snapshot, so future small keeps should continue to demand cross-harness confirmation.
- Next recommended step: move back to `propagate()` / conflict-analysis work. `backtrack()` is materially cheaper now, so the next meaningful win still likely has to come from watched-clause traversal or ternary-heavy reason handling rather than more backtrack micro-work.

## 2026-03-20T09:54:21+01:00 - Kept clause-family profiler split for hot watch/reason traffic

- Objective: determine whether the remaining hot propagation and conflict-analysis work on the dense bottlenecks is coming mostly from original clauses or learnt clauses, so the next solver change can target the right side of the search.
- Hypothesis: if the hot ternary traffic is mostly from learnt clauses, database-retention or learnt-layout work may still have real upside; if it is mostly from original clauses, the next win likely needs a raw ternary propagation / reason-traversal speedup instead.
- Files changed: `tools/profile_solver.py`, `tests/test_profile_solver.py`, `agent_log.md`, `experiments.jsonl`, `benchmark_summary.md`, `next_steps.md`
- Tests executed: `python -m unittest discover -s tests -v`; `python tools/profile_solver.py large/test_6.cnf special/hard.cnf medium/test_4.cnf large/test_8.cnf`
- Benchmark results: the upgraded profiler now splits active watch visits and reason traversals by clause family. On the dense pure-3-SAT hotspots, active ternary watch traffic is overwhelmingly from original clauses: `large/test_6.cnf` had `7,596,700` problem-clause ternary visits versus only `308,149` learnt-clause ternary visits (`96.10%` problem), `special/hard.cnf` had `4,194,393` versus `232,728` (`94.74%` problem), `medium/test_4.cnf` had `1,062,253` versus `50,847` (`95.43%` problem), and `large/test_8.cnf` had `1,976,129` versus `69,289` (`96.61%` problem). By contrast, the active large-clause watch path on those same instances is entirely learnt-clause traffic (`problem_large_visits=0` on all four cases). Conflict analysis also leans problem-clause-heavy: `analyze()` reason traversals were `887,029` problem versus `170,817` learnt on `large/test_6.cnf`, `618,633` versus `151,406` on `special/hard.cnf`, `150,818` versus `30,966` on `medium/test_4.cnf`, and `292,270` versus `45,266` on `large/test_8.cnf`. One-hop minimization reason checks showed the same direction, though less extremely (`63.62%`, `61.78%`, `63.05%`, and `74.72%` problem-clause respectively).
- Decision taken: keep the profiler/test upgrade and leave the production solver unchanged. The new data materially narrows the search space: on the current dense bottlenecks, the main ternary hot path is mostly original-formula work, while large-clause work is a learnt-clause story.
- Next recommended step: prioritize a solver change that removes raw original-clause ternary propagation / reason-traversal overhead. Broad learnt-retention policy work looks less likely to move the main UNSAT bottlenecks now, though learnt large-clause behavior still matters more on SAT-like `large/test_8.cnf`.

## 2026-03-20T10:18:20+01:00 - Kept watcher-side learnt-only deleted check with caution

- Objective: test whether the watched-clause hot path can skip an always-false branch for original clauses now that the new profiler says active ternary work is overwhelmingly problem-clause traffic.
- Hypothesis: because `reduce_database()` only deletes learnt clauses, changing the watched-clause loop from `if clause.deleted` to `if clause.learnt and clause.deleted` should shave a little propagation overhead from the dominant original-clause ternary path without changing the search.
- Files changed: `satsolver.py`, `tools/profile_solver.py`, `tests/test_solver_regressions.py`, `agent_log.md`, `experiments.jsonl`, `benchmark_summary.md`, `next_steps.md`, `out_extended.txt`, `out_cli_extended.txt`
- Tests executed: out-of-tree serial five-case A/B in forward and reverse order over `large/test_6.cnf`, `special/hard.cnf`, `medium/test_4.cnf`, `large/test_10.cnf`, and `large/test_8.cnf`; out-of-tree profiled A/B on `large/test_6.cnf`, `special/hard.cnf`, and `large/test_8.cnf`; `python -m unittest discover -s tests -v`; `python satsolver.py small/test_1.cnf /tmp/cycle_sat.txt`; `python tools/checker.py small/test_1.cnf /tmp/cycle_sat.txt`; `python satsolver.py special/tseitin.cnf /tmp/cycle_unsat.txt`; `python tools/checker.py special/tseitin.cnf /tmp/cycle_unsat.txt --bruteforce-var-limit 0`; multiple `python benchmark_suite.py ... --repeat 2` module and exact-CLI runs on both the experimental solver and a temporary reverted-baseline script; exact-CLI five-case A/B in forward and reverse order using `python satsolver.py input.cnf output.txt` against a temporary reverted-baseline script.
- Benchmark results: the direct same-search hotspot signal was positive in both orders. On the serial five-case slice, the baseline totals were `30.9099s` forward and `31.1534s` reverse, while the experimental totals were `30.4341s` and `29.4951s`, improving the order-averaged total from `31.0317s` to `29.9646s` (`-3.44%`). The exact required CLI path on that same five-case slice also stayed positive in both orders, improving from `25.7390s` and `26.1964s` to `25.4543s` and `26.1397s`, for an average `25.9677s -> 25.7970s` (`-0.66%`). The profiled comparison preserved identical search counts on `large/test_6.cnf`, `special/hard.cnf`, and `large/test_8.cnf`, but was slower under instrumentation (`20.7688s -> 21.4601s`, `13.7854s -> 14.9141s`, and `5.9403s -> 6.5875s` respectively), so this is not a profiler-friendly change even though the uninstrumented A/B remained positive. Repeat-aware full-suite runs were much noisier: experimental runs landed at `29.1333s` and later `30.3709s` in-process plus `31.5217s` and later `32.8080s` exact CLI, while same-day reverted-baseline guard reruns landed at `29.8019s` in-process and `31.8364s` exact CLI. Those full-suite numbers were too unstable to compare directly against older archived snapshots, so the keep decision is driven by the same-day hotspot A/B data plus the exact-CLI hotspot confirmation rather than by historical full-suite comparisons.
- Decision taken: keep the change, but explicitly as a small A/B-backed propagation win with noisy full-suite repeat2 artifacts. I also kept a new invariant regression asserting that `reduce_database()` never deletes problem clauses, because that is the assumption the optimization relies on.
- Next recommended step: treat this as another reminder that sub-1% propagation tweaks need direct same-day baseline-vs-experiment A/B, preferably on the exact CLI hotspot slice, instead of comparisons against older archived repeat-aware full-suite outputs. The next solver idea should still aim for a more structural ternary propagation / reason-traversal reduction rather than stacking more bookkeeping micro-branches.

The profiled comparison showed the search path itself stayed fixed on the three main cases: `large/test_6.cnf` kept `72,886` decisions, `59,201` conflicts, and `2,431,409` processed literals, `special/hard.cnf` kept `54,245` decisions, `44,619` conflicts, and `1,737,825` processed literals, and `large/test_8.cnf` kept `21,526` decisions, `16,430` conflicts, and `838,303` processed literals. Despite that, the profiled wall times were mixed and net worse overall (`40.6062s -> 40.7185s` across those three cases), so this branch was another pure-overhead `analyze()` loser rather than a search improvement.
- Decision taken: discard the branch and leave the production solver unchanged. The current keep from the watched-clause path remains the retained solver baseline.
- Next recommended step: do not revisit hand-unrolled problem-clause ternary handling inside `analyze()` unless the change removes more work than this branch did. The next useful conflict-analysis idea still needs a stronger structural simplification than manually spelling out the three-literal loop.

## 2026-03-20T12:00:53+01:00 - Rejected raw-`fork` portfolio plumbing from `maybe.md`

On repeated exact-CLI `large/test_8.cnf` runs, the baseline script averaged `0.2892s` and the raw-`fork` patched script averaged `0.2626s`, with medians `0.2889s -> 0.2591s`. But the same-day repeat-aware exact-CLI 59-case suite still favored the retained baseline overall: baseline `27.1974s` total, `54.3948s` measured, `54.6329s` wall clock, versus raw-`fork` `27.8860s`, `55.7719s`, `56.0236s`. `large/test_8.cnf` itself improved inside those suite runs (`0.2909s -> 0.2823s`), but the aggregate exact-CLI measurement still regressed, so the single-case win was not enough to justify the change.
- Decision taken: discard the raw-`fork` portfolio rewrite and restore the retained solver. I also kept the same-day retained exact-CLI benchmark artifact (`27.1974s`) because it is a cleaner current snapshot of the unchanged solver than the older `27.3138s` file.
- Next recommended step: treat portfolio implementation churn as lower priority than solver-core work unless a parallelism change shows a broad same-day exact-CLI win, not just a better `large/test_8.cnf`. `maybe.md` is still directionally right that stdlib parallelism is the only legal concurrency lever, but the remaining gains there are too narrow and noisy compared with the dense UNSAT bottlenecks.

But the same-day repeat-aware exact-CLI 59-case suite regressed from the retained `27.1974s` to `28.2602s` (`+3.91%`), with the worst case growing from `11.5797s` to `12.2066s` on `large/test_6.cnf`. - Next recommended step: keep looking for helper-boundary or bookkeeping removals inside `analyze()`, but do not assume every such cleanup is a free win. For changes of this size, demand same-day exact-CLI suite confirmation before keeping them, even if the module hotspot slice looks modestly positive.

## 2026-03-20T12:07:39+01:00 - Rejected `solve()` helper-call cleanup around decay and reduction

- Benchmark results: the partial and combined branches both stayed too noisy to justify a keep. The stronger combined `solve()` cleanup looked a bit better on the serial five-case slice, averaging `26.6212s -> 26.4263s` (`-0.73%`), but the exact-CLI hotspot slice was effectively tied: forward order improved from `23.4780s` to `23.0781s`, reverse order regressed from `23.3016s` to `23.6969s`, for an order-averaged `23.3898s -> 23.3875s` (`-0.01%`). That is not strong enough to justify a real keep, especially without a clean exact-CLI full-suite lead.

- Next recommended step: treat `solve()`-level helper pruning as another marginal path unless a future branch produces a clearly positive exact-CLI hotspot result before the full-suite gate. The profiled comparison preserved exact search counts on `large/test_6.cnf`, `special/hard.cnf`, and `large/test_8.cnf` while moving wall time around family-by-family. A temporary exact-CLI wrapper run looked modestly positive in both orders (`25.0929s -> 25.0728s` forward, `25.5556s -> 24.8564s` reverse), but the stricter same-day repo-vs-saved-baseline exact-CLI hotspot A/B failed to hold: forward order improved from `25.9713s` to `25.5458s`, reverse order regressed from `25.5206s` to `26.3185s`, for an average `25.7459s -> 25.9322s` (`+0.72%`). The reverse-order losses hit `large/test_10.cnf` (`1.7733s -> 2.0426s`), `medium/test_4.cnf` (`1.8304s -> 2.1892s`), and `large/test_8.cnf` (`0.3157s -> 0.3499s`) hard enough that the branch is not trustworthy.
- Decision taken: discard the branch and restore the retained solver baseline. Even though the profiled hard cases kept identical decisions, conflicts, and processed-literal counts, the real exact-CLI A/B stayed too order-sensitive and leaned negative once rerun against the saved baseline script.
- Next recommended step: treat simple unit-vs-conflict branch-order flips on the watched no-replacement tail as another propagation dead end. The next useful propagation idea still needs to remove more actual ternary traversal work than just swapping rare-tail branches.

## 2026-03-20T12:45:44+01:00 - Rejected static phase-bias watch ordering for problem ternary clauses

- Objective: test whether the dominant original-clause ternary workload can be improved structurally by reordering each problem ternary clause once at ingest time, instead of changing the watched-literal hot loop itself.
- Hypothesis: because the current bottleneck is overwhelmingly original-clause ternary traffic, sorting each ternary problem clause so the two most phase-bias-aligned literals are watched might reduce future watched-clause work enough to beat the retained baseline.
- Files changed: `agent_log.md`, `experiments.jsonl`, `benchmark_summary.md`, `next_steps.md`
- Tests executed: reread `autonomous_sat_agent_prompt.md`, `next_steps.md`, and `benchmark_summary.md`; out-of-tree serial five-case A/B for both `WatchBestScoreSolver` and `CandidateBestScoreSolver` over `large/test_6.cnf`, `special/hard.cnf`, `medium/test_4.cnf`, `large/test_10.cnf`, and `large/test_8.cnf` in forward and reverse order; out-of-tree exact-CLI hotspot A/B comparing `/tmp/satsolver_watch_best_candidate.py` against the retained `satsolver.py`; `python /tmp/satsolver_watch_best_candidate.py small/test_1.cnf /tmp/watchbest_sat.txt`; `python tools/checker.py small/test_1.cnf /tmp/watchbest_sat.txt`; `python /tmp/satsolver_watch_best_candidate.py special/tseitin.cnf /tmp/watchbest_unsat.txt`; `python tools/checker.py special/tseitin.cnf /tmp/watchbest_unsat.txt --bruteforce-var-limit 0`; `python /tmp/satsolver_watch_best_candidate.py large/test_8.cnf /tmp/watchbest_large8.txt`; `python tools/checker.py large/test_8.cnf /tmp/watchbest_large8.txt`
- Benchmark results: the clause-order family was real, but too family-specific to keep. Sorting ternary problem clauses so the most phase-bias-aligned literals were watched (`WatchBestScoreSolver`) improved the serial five-case slice in both orders, from `26.7563s -> 24.3859s` forward and `26.2390s -> 24.7625s` reverse, mostly by crushing serial `large/test_8.cnf` (`3.3530s -> 0.0956s`, `3.2034s -> 0.1022s`) and helping `special/hard.cnf` (`7.8833s -> 6.4533s`, `7.6190s -> 6.7534s`). But it also made `large/test_6.cnf` and `large/test_10.cnf` materially worse (`12.1716s -> 13.5418s`, `1.6812s -> 2.7040s` forward). The opposite heuristic, putting the highest-score literal in the unwatched candidate slot (`CandidateBestScoreSolver`), was catastrophic on both orders (`26.3339s -> 38.4724s` forward, `26.8103s -> 37.6784s` reverse). The stronger gate failed too: on the exact required CLI path with portfolio enabled, the seemingly-good `WatchBestScoreSolver` still regressed overall from `23.7741s` to `24.4516s` forward and from `23.4966s` to `26.4515s` reverse. It made `large/test_8.cnf` even faster there (`0.3069s -> 0.1726s`, `0.3029s -> 0.1885s`) and helped `special/hard.cnf`, but it lost much more on `large/test_6.cnf` and `large/test_10.cnf`, so the actual submission-path signal is clearly negative.
- Decision taken: discard the clause-ordering branch and leave the production solver unchanged. The temporary candidate stayed checker-clean on SAT and UNSAT spot checks, but the exact-CLI hotspot gate failed too hard to justify a real patch.
- Next recommended step: treat raw phase-bias-driven static watch ordering for ternary problem clauses as another family-specific dead end. If clause-order heuristics are revisited, they need a much better classifier than raw signed phase bias alone and must be checked against the current exact-CLI baseline, not just the serial hotspot slice.

It did help `large/test_6.cnf` in both orders (`13.6153s -> 13.1510s`, `13.7927s -> 13.2743s`), but it made `special/hard.cnf`, `medium/test_4.cnf`, `large/test_10.cnf`, and `large/test_8.cnf` worse, including `large/test_8.cnf 3.4821s -> 3.7609s` forward and `3.5502s -> 4.0964s` reverse.

It was basically flat on `large/test_8.cnf`, mixed on `large/test_10.cnf`, and too inconsistent on the main UNSAT cases to justify keeping.

## 2026-03-20T13:15:51+01:00 - Rejected blocker cache for original ternary problem clauses

- Objective: test whether the dominant original-clause ternary traffic can skip more watched-slot work by caching one known-satisfied literal per ternary problem clause and checking it before the usual ternary propagation path.
- Hypothesis: because the hot benchmarks spend millions of visits in original ternary clauses, a per-clause blocker literal might let satisfied clauses bail out before normalization and other watched-literal bookkeeping, cutting real propagation cost without changing the search path.
- Files changed: `agent_log.md`, `experiments.jsonl`, `benchmark_summary.md`, `next_steps.md`
- Tests executed: reread `autonomous_sat_agent_prompt.md`, `next_steps.md`, `agent_log.md`, and `benchmark_summary.md`; duplicate-clause scan on the five hotspot cases and on the full 59-case corpus (both found `0` duplicate clauses); refreshed `tools/profile_solver.py` on `large/test_6.cnf`, `special/hard.cnf`, `medium/test_4.cnf`, and `large/test_8.cnf`; refreshed `cProfile` on `large/test_6.cnf`, `special/hard.cnf`, and `large/test_8.cnf`; built and ran a temporary out-of-tree `ProblemTernaryBlockerSolver` via `PYTHONPATH=. python scratch/exp_problem_ternary_blocker.py` over `large/test_6.cnf`, `special/hard.cnf`, `medium/test_4.cnf`, `large/test_10.cnf`, and `large/test_8.cnf` in forward and reverse order; deleted the scratch experiment script after measurement; `python -m unittest discover -s tests -v`; `python satsolver.py small/test_1.cnf /tmp/cycle_sat.txt`; `python tools/checker.py small/test_1.cnf /tmp/cycle_sat.txt`; `python satsolver.py special/tseitin.cnf /tmp/cycle_unsat.txt`; `python tools/checker.py special/tseitin.cnf /tmp/cycle_unsat.txt --bruteforce-var-limit 0`
- Benchmark results: the blocker idea was decisively bad and did not earn an exact-CLI follow-up. On the serial five-case slice it exploded from `28.6845s` to `64.5447s` in forward order and from `31.2633s` to `71.4741s` in reverse order. The biggest loss was SAT-like `large/test_8.cnf`, which blew up from `3.5694s` to `22.7884s` forward and from `3.4577s` to `24.5450s` reverse, but the hard UNSAT cases were hit badly too: `large/test_6.cnf 12.8951s -> 24.4723s` forward and `14.8364s -> 26.9603s` reverse, `special/hard.cnf 8.5803s -> 12.8553s` forward and `9.3545s -> 15.0191s` reverse. The duplicate-clause scan also showed `0` duplicate clauses across the benchmark corpus, so duplicate-elimination preprocessing is not the next win either.
- Decision taken: discard the blocker-cache branch and leave the production solver unchanged. This is now a strong negative data point against naive blocker-style satisfied-literal caches for original ternary clauses in pure Python.
- Next recommended step: keep looking for a structural win that removes real original-ternary relocation/unit/conflict work, not a blocker-style side cache or another low-signal preprocessing cleanup.

The structural screen also said complementary-ternary-to-binary preprocessing is too sparse on the main bottlenecks to prioritize right now: `0` derived binary pairs on `large/test_6.cnf`, `special/hard.cnf`, `medium/test_4.cnf`, and `large/test_8.cnf`, and only `1` on `large/test_10.cnf`.

## 2026-03-20T13:34:18+01:00 - Rejected fixed ternary trigger network for problem clauses

- Objective: test whether the dominant original-clause ternary work can be cut structurally by removing watched-literal mutation entirely for problem 3-clauses and handling them through fixed per-literal ternary triggers instead.
- Hypothesis: because the profiler says the hard pure-3-SAT bottlenecks are overwhelmingly original-clause ternary traffic, replacing watched propagation for those clauses with a static “if this literal is false, inspect the other two” trigger table might remove normalization, relocation, and watcher-list churn without hurting correctness.
- Files changed: `agent_log.md`, `experiments.jsonl`, `benchmark_summary.md`, `next_steps.md`
- Tests executed: reread `autonomous_sat_agent_prompt.md`, `next_steps.md`, `agent_log.md`, and `benchmark_summary.md`; built and ran a temporary out-of-tree `ProblemTernaryTriggerSolver`; small SAT/UNSAT scratch correctness checks on `small/test_1.cnf` and `special/tseitin.cnf`; same-day serial hotspot forward A/B on `large/test_6.cnf`, `special/hard.cnf`, `medium/test_4.cnf`, `large/test_10.cnf`, and `large/test_8.cnf`; deleted the scratch experiment script after measurement; `python -m unittest discover -s tests -v`; `python satsolver.py small/test_1.cnf /tmp/cycle_sat.txt`; `python tools/checker.py small/test_1.cnf /tmp/cycle_sat.txt`; `python satsolver.py special/tseitin.cnf /tmp/cycle_unsat.txt`; `python tools/checker.py special/tseitin.cnf /tmp/cycle_unsat.txt --bruteforce-var-limit 0`
- Benchmark results: the branch failed decisively on the first same-day hotspot gate, so it did not earn a reverse-order or exact-CLI follow-up. The forward five-case serial slice regressed from `29.8075s` to `57.8977s` (`+94.24%`). It slightly improved `medium/test_4.cnf` (`1.8705s -> 1.7459s`) but made every more important case worse: `large/test_6.cnf 13.6006s -> 14.6982s`, `special/hard.cnf 8.8652s -> 11.2639s`, `large/test_10.cnf 1.8093s -> 1.8434s`, and especially `large/test_8.cnf 3.6618s -> 28.3463s`. The search path also got much worse instead of just running the same work more slowly: conflicts rose `59,201 -> 64,397` on `large/test_6.cnf`, `44,619 -> 55,387` on `special/hard.cnf`, `9,650 -> 10,495` on `large/test_10.cnf`, and `16,430 -> 113,573` on `large/test_8.cnf`.
- Decision taken: discard the fixed-trigger branch and leave the production solver unchanged. This is a strong negative result against replacing watched propagation for original ternary problem clauses with a static per-literal trigger network in pure Python.
- Next recommended step: if original ternary propagation is revisited, preserve more of watched-literal selectivity. A fixed “visit every false-literal incidence” network is too blunt on this workload, even though the workload is mostly 3-SAT.

## 2026-03-20T13:41:57+01:00 - Kept clause-concentration profiling for original ternary traffic

- Objective: answer whether the remaining original-ternary hotspot work is concentrated on a few pathological clauses or spread diffusely across most problem clauses, so the next solver cycle can stop guessing between clause-specific tricks and broader hot-loop work.
- Hypothesis: if the hard cases repeatedly hammer only a small subset of original ternary problem clauses or problem-clause reasons, a clause-targeted cache or ordering rule might still be viable; if coverage is near-total and the hottest share is tiny, future work should favor broad propagation/conflict-analysis simplifications instead.

- Benchmark results: the new profiler says the hotspot traffic is highly diffuse, not concentrated. On `large/test_6.cnf`, all `1,393 / 1,393` problem ternary clauses were visited, and the hottest single clause accounted for only `12,368 / 7,596,700` problem-ternary visits (`0.16%`); problem-clause reasons were similarly broad at `1,386` distinct clauses with a hottest share of `0.34%`. `special/hard.cnf` showed `850 / 850` clause coverage with hottest problem-ternary share `0.26%` and hottest problem-reason share `0.54%`; `medium/test_4.cnf` showed `886 / 886` with `0.26%` and `0.60%`; `large/test_8.cnf` showed `1,210 / 1,210` with `0.20%` and `0.49%`. So the remaining original-ternary work is spread across almost every problem 3-clause rather than being dominated by a tiny pathological subset.
- Decision taken: keep the profiler/test upgrade. The production solver remains unchanged, but the repo now has a much sharper boundary on future work: broad hot-loop savings are more promising than clause-specific caches or clause-ordering tricks on the main bottlenecks.

## 2026-03-20T14:13:22+01:00 - Rejected simple dense-UNSAT alternate-worker sweep

- Objective: test whether the remaining stdlib-portfolio angle has a viable second worker for the long dense pure-3-SAT UNSAT cases, instead of only for the SAT-like `large/test_8.cnf` family.
- Hypothesis: because the current portfolio gate only helps the lower-density SAT-like case, a second worker with slightly different restart or decay settings might beat the default search on the denser pure-3-SAT hotspot family (`large/test_6.cnf`, `special/hard.cnf`, `medium/test_4.cnf`, `large/test_10.cnf`) strongly enough to justify a future density-based UNSAT portfolio gate.
- Files changed: `agent_log.md`, `experiments.jsonl`, `benchmark_summary.md`, `next_steps.md`
- Tests executed: reread `autonomous_sat_agent_prompt.md`, `next_steps.md`, recent `agent_log.md`, and `benchmark_summary.md`; refreshed `tools/profile_solver.py` on `large/test_6.cnf`, `special/hard.cnf`, `medium/test_4.cnf`, and `large/test_8.cnf`; refreshed `cProfile` on `large/test_6.cnf` and `special/hard.cnf`; formula-feature scan on `large/test_6.cnf`, `special/hard.cnf`, `medium/test_4.cnf`, `large/test_10.cnf`, and `large/test_8.cnf`; out-of-tree serial worker sweep across those five cases for `default`, `restart_base=32`, `restart_base=128`, `var_decay=0.94`, `var_decay=0.96`, and `all_true`; `python -m unittest discover -s tests -v`; `python satsolver.py small/test_1.cnf /tmp/cycle_sat.txt`; `python tools/checker.py small/test_1.cnf /tmp/cycle_sat.txt`; `python satsolver.py special/tseitin.cnf /tmp/cycle_unsat.txt`; `python tools/checker.py special/tseitin.cnf /tmp/cycle_unsat.txt --bruteforce-var-limit 0`
- Benchmark results: the case split is real but the alternate workers were not. The hotspot formulas are all pure 3-SAT, with densities `5.1402` (`large/test_6.cnf`), `4.2500` (`special/hard.cnf`), `4.6387` (`medium/test_4.cnf`), `5.5895` (`large/test_10.cnf`), and `4.0604` (`large/test_8.cnf`). But the worker sweep still favored the default search overall at `28.9875s`: `restart_base=32` regressed to `36.5529s`, `restart_base=128` to `46.3689s`, `var_decay=0.94` to `35.6389s`, `var_decay=0.96` to `31.1810s`, and `all_true` to `31.5186s`. The tempting variants still behaved like narrow `large/test_8.cnf` specialists rather than dense-UNSAT specialists: `var_decay=0.94` cut `large/test_8.cnf` from `3.7694s` to `0.3785s` and `all_true` cut it to `0.2104s`, but both made `large/test_6.cnf` and `special/hard.cnf` much worse. Meanwhile the one “least bad” dense variant, `var_decay=0.96`, still lost because `special/hard.cnf` blew up from `8.6136s` to `11.4953s`.
- Decision taken: discard the dense-UNSAT alternate-worker idea for now and leave the production solver unchanged. The current portfolio still has one justified niche, `large/test_8.cnf`, but simple restart/decay/phase variants do not provide a second worker strong enough for the denser UNSAT-heavy family.
- Next recommended step: return to solver-core work. If portfolio work is revisited again, it needs a materially different alternate search identity than local restart-base, local decay tuning, or fixed-phase seeding.

## 2026-03-20T15:00:25+01:00 - Kept gated root pure-literal presolve

- Objective: test whether iterative root pure-literal elimination is useful on the real corpus, and if so, keep only a version that helps the submission path instead of blindly perturbing the dense hotspot search paths.
- Hypothesis: a root-only iterative pure-literal presolve may cheaply shrink the formulas that actually expose pure literals, but tiny one-literal fixes may be more likely to perturb the search path than to pay for themselves on the dense hotspot family, so any keep may need a gate rather than an always-on rule.
- Files changed: `satsolver.py`, `tools/profile_solver.py`, `tests/test_solver_regressions.py`, `tests/test_profile_solver.py`, `agent_log.md`, `experiments.jsonl`, `benchmark_summary.md`, `next_steps.md`, `out_extended.txt`, `out_cli_extended.txt`
- Tests executed: reread `autonomous_sat_agent_prompt.md`, `next_steps.md`, `satsolver.py`, and recent logs; scanned the full 59-case corpus for iterative root pure literals; took a same-day exact-CLI baseline on a mixed slice of pure-hit cases plus hotspot cases; implemented iterative root pure-literal discovery from normalized clauses and initially wired it in unconditionally; fixed a profiler regression fixture that the new presolve simplified away; measured the ungated branch on the same mixed exact-CLI slice; tightened the solver and profiler to apply the presolve only when the fixpoint reaches at least two assignments; `python -m py_compile satsolver.py tools/profile_solver.py tests/test_solver_regressions.py tests/test_profile_solver.py`; `python -m unittest discover -s tests -v`; `python satsolver.py small/test_1.cnf /tmp/pure_cycle_sat.txt`; `python tools/checker.py small/test_1.cnf /tmp/pure_cycle_sat.txt`; `python satsolver.py special/tseitin.cnf /tmp/pure_cycle_unsat.txt`; `python tools/checker.py special/tseitin.cnf /tmp/pure_cycle_unsat.txt --bruteforce-var-limit 0`; reran the same exact-CLI mixed slice for both the ungated and gated versions; `python benchmark_suite.py satsolver out_extended.txt small medium large special satlib_subset satlib_more --bruteforce-var-limit 16 --repeat 2`; `python benchmark_suite.py satsolver out_cli_extended.txt small medium large special satlib_subset satlib_more --bruteforce-var-limit 16 --cli-script satsolver.py --repeat 2`; reran the exact-CLI repeat-aware suite once more to check whether the full-suite CLI signal was drifting.
- Benchmark results: the corpus scan found iterative root pure literals on `16 / 59` instances, totaling `285` root assignments, including large simplifications on `large/test_1.cnf` (`51`), `large/test_2.cnf` (`80`), `large/test_7.cnf` (`38`), and `special/easy.cnf` (`50`). The ungated branch was a clear miss on the same-day exact-CLI mixed slice: it regressed from `22.2586s` to `25.1953s`, mostly because single-pure hotspot cases blew up (`large/test_6.cnf 11.8230s -> 14.8664s`, `large/test_8.cnf 0.2907s -> 0.3593s`). Gating the presolve at `>= 2` root assignments recovered that and improved the same slice to `22.0635s`, with `large/test_6.cnf` back at `11.9292s`, `large/test_8.cnf` back at `0.2873s`, and several real pure-hit cases faster (`large/test_1.cnf 0.0540s -> 0.0446s`, `large/test_2.cnf 0.0497s -> 0.0409s`, `special/easy.cnf 0.0509s -> 0.0379s`). The repeat-aware in-process full suite improved from `25.2549s` to `25.1541s`, which is a new local best there. The repeat-aware exact-CLI full suite stayed mixed: two reruns landed at `27.7868s` and `27.6136s`, so the historical `27.1974s` exact-CLI best still stands.
- Decision taken: keep the gated version. The ungated presolve is now a measured dead end, but the `>= 2`-assignment gate is strong enough to keep: it improves same-day exact-CLI A/B on the intended mixed slice, improves the repeat-aware in-process 59-case suite, keeps `59/59` correct in both validated benchmark modes, and turns the pure-literal idea into a bounded, evidence-backed presolve rather than a blanket rule.
- Next recommended step: if presolve work is revisited, keep it tightly classified and demand same-day exact-CLI evidence before broadening it. Single-literal root simplifications should be treated as likely search-path perturbations first, not default wins.

## 2026-03-20T15:11:38+01:00 - Rejected raising the root pure-literal gate to `>= 5`

- Objective: see whether the new root pure-literal presolve can be tightened further so it helps the exact required CLI path rather than only the in-process repeat-aware suite.
- Hypothesis: because the current `>= 2` gate still admits a few low-shrink pure-hit cases, raising the gate to `>= 5` root pure assignments might keep the big simplification wins while trimming more of the search-path perturbation risk on the full exact-CLI suite.
- Files changed: `agent_log.md`, `experiments.jsonl`, `benchmark_summary.md`, `next_steps.md`
- Tests executed: reread `autonomous_sat_agent_prompt.md`, `next_steps.md`, `agent_log.md`, and `benchmark_summary.md`; computed per-case root pure assignment counts and clause-removal fractions across the 59-case corpus; benchmarked out-of-tree threshold variants (`>= 2`, `>= 5`, `>= 10`, `>= 20`) on the exact-CLI pure-hit slice using temporary `/tmp/satsolver_rootpure_*.py` copies; benchmarked `>= 2` vs `>= 5` on the same-day exact-CLI mixed slice that also includes `large/test_6.cnf`, `special/hard.cnf`, `medium/test_4.cnf`, and `large/test_8.cnf`; ran a full repeat-aware exact-CLI 59-case suite on `/tmp/satsolver_rootpure_5.py`; `python -m unittest discover -s tests -v` on the retained production solver.
- Benchmark results: the slice signals were tempting but the suite-level gate failed. On the exact-CLI pure-hit slice, `>= 5` beat the retained `>= 2` gate `0.4217s -> 0.4591s`. On the mixed same-day exact-CLI slice, `>= 5` also won `22.1814s -> 22.5906s`, helped by faster pure-hit cases and a modest gain on `large/test_6.cnf` (`12.0150s` vs `12.1779s`). But the real repeat-aware exact-CLI 59-case suite still lost overall at `27.7944s`, which is worse than both the current retained same-day rerun `27.6136s` and the historical best `27.1974s`. So a larger assignment-count gate alone is not a reliable full-suite improvement.
- Decision taken: discard the `>= 5` gate and leave the production solver unchanged on the current `>= 2` baseline. The useful outcome is the boundary, not a code keep: slice-local wins from pure-literal gate tuning are not trustworthy enough by themselves.
- Next recommended step: if root pure-literal gating is revisited again, use a classifier stronger than assignment count alone, and still require a repeat-aware exact-CLI full-suite win before changing the retained gate.

## 2026-03-20T15:11:38+01:00 - Rejected first-round pure-literal screening prescan

- Objective: test whether the current gated root pure-literal presolve can be made cheaper by doing a very cheap first-round purity screen and only running the full iterative fixpoint when that screen says the case is promising.
- Hypothesis: because every currently useful corpus case with total root pure assignments `>= 2` already has at least two first-round pure literals, a first-round purity screen might skip the full iterative scan on most no-op cases without sacrificing the real current presolve wins.
- Files changed: `agent_log.md`, `experiments.jsonl`, `benchmark_summary.md`, `next_steps.md`
- Tests executed: reread `autonomous_sat_agent_prompt.md`, `next_steps.md`, `agent_log.md`, and `benchmark_summary.md`; measured first-round pure counts versus total iterative pure counts across the 59-case corpus; built temporary `/tmp/satsolver_rootpure_initialscreen.py` that adds an extra first-round purity prescan before the current iterative presolve; benchmarked it against the retained production solver on the same-day exact-CLI mixed slice; ran a full repeat-aware exact-CLI 59-case suite on the temporary script; `python -m unittest discover -s tests -v` on the retained production solver.
- Benchmark results: the prediction signal was clean but the added pass still lost. On the corpus, every currently useful root-pure case with total assignments `>= 2` already had at least two first-round pures, while the one-pure cases (`small/test_7.cnf`, `large/test_6.cnf`, `large/test_8.cnf`, `satlib_more/uf125-01.cnf`) never grew past one. The temporary prescan looked mildly positive on the same-day exact-CLI mixed slice (`23.4612s -> 23.3365s`). But the real repeat-aware exact-CLI 59-case suite regressed badly to `29.3678s`, far worse than both the retained current gate’s recent rerun `27.6136s` and the historical best `27.1974s`. So the extra whole-formula screen costs more than it pays back on the full submission path.
- Decision taken: discard the first-round prescan and leave the production solver unchanged. The key result is another anti-cherrypicking boundary: even a corpus-perfect screen can still be a net loss if it adds another full normalization/polarity pass over every instance.
- Next recommended step: if the root pure-literal gate is revisited again, avoid extra whole-formula prescans unless they remove substantially more work than they cost. Future classifiers should probably piggyback on work the solver is already doing rather than adding another front-loaded pass.

## 2026-03-20T15:30:04+01:00 - Kept large-watch size-bucket profiling

- Objective: determine whether the remaining learnt large-clause watch traffic is concentrated enough in 4-literal clauses to justify a narrow large-clause fast path, or whether it is spread broadly enough that such a fast path would miss most of the real work.
- Hypothesis: if active large watched-clause visits are mostly 4-literal, a 4-literal-specific propagation idea might still be worth prototyping; if the traffic is split across `4`, `5..9`, and `10+`, then future large-clause work needs a broader size-aware story and should not assume a tiny fast path will cover the dominant workload.
- Files changed: `tools/profile_solver.py`, `tests/test_profile_solver.py`, `agent_log.md`, `experiments.jsonl`, `benchmark_summary.md`, `next_steps.md`
- Tests executed: reread `autonomous_sat_agent_prompt.md`, `next_steps.md`, the latest `agent_log.md`, and `benchmark_summary.md`; refreshed `cProfile` on `large/test_6.cnf`; reran `python tools/profile_solver.py large/test_6.cnf special/hard.cnf medium/test_4.cnf large/test_8.cnf` on the retained baseline; extended `tools/profile_solver.py` to bucket large watched-clause visits into `4`, `5..9`, and `10+`; updated `tests/test_profile_solver.py`; `python -m py_compile tools/profile_solver.py tests/test_profile_solver.py`; `python -m unittest discover -s tests -v`; reran `python tools/profile_solver.py large/test_6.cnf special/hard.cnf medium/test_4.cnf large/test_8.cnf`.
- Benchmark results: the new split says the active large-clause path is not dominated by 4-literal clauses. On `large/test_6.cnf`, large-watch visits split `638,300` (`32.91%`) for length `4`, `761,325` (`39.25%`) for `5..9`, and `539,866` (`27.84%`) for `10+`. `special/hard.cnf` split `526,229` (`30.50%`), `764,961` (`44.34%`), and `433,907` (`25.15%`). `medium/test_4.cnf` split `56,771` (`24.73%`), `103,916` (`45.28%`), and `68,834` (`29.99%`). `large/test_8.cnf` was the one heavier-tail outlier, with `85,215` (`25.35%`), `115,147` (`34.25%`), and `135,806` (`40.40%`). The refreshed `cProfile` still had `propagate()` as the main cost on `large/test_6.cnf` at `14.348s` tottime, with `analyze()` next at `2.331s`, so the profiler upgrade reinforces that the next real move is still propagation-driven.
- Decision taken: keep the profiler/test upgrade. The production solver remains unchanged, but the repo now has a clearer boundary against assuming a 4-literal learnt-clause fast path would cover most of the remaining large-clause work.
- Next recommended step: if the large-clause path is revisited, prototype something that either helps both the `5..9` bucket and the `10+` tail or that is explicitly justified against `large/test_8.cnf`; do not assume a narrow 4-literal specialization is the main remaining learnt-large win.

## 2026-03-20T15:42:35+01:00 - Rejected manual first-candidate large-scan unroll

- Objective: test whether the learnt large-clause watch path can be sped up by manually handling the first one or two replacement candidates before falling back to the general scan loop, now that the profiler says average large-clause probe depth is only about `2.3..3.4`.
- Hypothesis: because large replacement scans usually succeed or fail within the first few candidates, manually unrolling the first `2` scan steps while preserving candidate order might remove enough Python loop overhead to help the hotspot slice without changing the search path.
- Files changed: `agent_log.md`, `experiments.jsonl`, `benchmark_summary.md`, `next_steps.md`
- Tests executed: reread `autonomous_sat_agent_prompt.md`, `agent_log.md`, `benchmark_summary.md`, and `next_steps.md`; refreshed `cProfile` on `special/hard.cnf`; reran `python tools/profile_solver.py large/test_6.cnf special/hard.cnf medium/test_4.cnf large/test_8.cnf`; built then removed a temporary `scratch/exp_large_scan_unroll.py` harness implementing an out-of-tree `UnrolledLargeScanSolver`; `python -m py_compile scratch/exp_large_scan_unroll.py`; `PYTHONPATH=. python scratch/exp_large_scan_unroll.py`; `python -m unittest discover -s tests -v` on the retained production solver after removing the scratch harness.
- Benchmark results: the experimental branch preserved the exact same SAT/UNSAT outcomes and conflict counts on every hotspot case, so it was a pure overhead experiment rather than a search-path change. But it lost cleanly in both orders. Forward, the five-case serial slice regressed from `28.3822s` to `29.6272s` (`+1.2449s`), with `special/hard.cnf 8.3740s -> 8.6766s`, `large/test_10.cnf 1.6713s -> 2.0074s`, and `large/test_8.cnf 3.4652s -> 4.0545s`. Reverse, it regressed from `30.2405s` to `31.4678s` (`+1.2273s`), with the same exact conflict counts across all five cases and the biggest loss on `special/hard.cnf 9.0114s -> 10.0698s`.
- Decision taken: discard the manual unroll and leave the production solver unchanged. The useful result is the boundary: even on a path with short average probe depth, spelling out the first large-clause scan steps in Python added more overhead than it removed.
- Next recommended step: if the learnt large-clause path is revisited, do not assume that low average probe depth alone makes manual loop unrolling worthwhile. Future work there needs a broader structural simplification than “check candidates `2` and `3` inline first,” and it still needs to be justified specifically against `large/test_8.cnf`.

## 2026-03-20T15:50:56+01:00 - Kept large-clause success-depth profiler buckets

- Objective: refine the remaining learnt large-clause question after the failed scan-head unroll by measuring where successful replacement scans actually land, not just their average depth.
- Hypothesis: if most successful large-clause relocations really do happen in the first one or two probe steps, that would sharpen the boundary from the rejected manual unroll: head-heavy success distribution may be real, but any future large-clause optimization still needs to remove more than just Python loop-head overhead.
- Files changed: `tools/profile_solver.py`, `tests/test_profile_solver.py`, `agent_log.md`, `experiments.jsonl`, `benchmark_summary.md`, `next_steps.md`
- Tests executed: reread `autonomous_sat_agent_prompt.md`, `agent_log.md`, `benchmark_summary.md`, and `next_steps.md`; extended `tools/profile_solver.py` with success-depth buckets for large-clause relocations plus CLI reporting; added regression coverage in `tests/test_profile_solver.py`; `python -m py_compile tools/profile_solver.py tests/test_profile_solver.py`; `python -m unittest discover -s tests -v`; `python tools/profile_solver.py large/test_6.cnf special/hard.cnf medium/test_4.cnf large/test_8.cnf`.
- Benchmark results: the profiler upgrade says successful large-clause relocations are indeed front-loaded on the main hard cases, but not enough to rescue a manual Python unroll. On `large/test_6.cnf`, successful large relocations split `822,180` (`47.17%`) at probe step `1`, `495,302` (`28.42%`) at step `2`, `255,684` (`14.67%`) at steps `3..4`, and `169,899` (`9.75%`) at `5+`, so steps `1..2` account for `75.59%`. `special/hard.cnf` shows the same shape at `74.81%` in the first two steps (`697,056`, `451,738`, `252,094`, `134,760`). `medium/test_4.cnf` lands at `69.75%` in the first two steps (`83,754`, `56,442`, `37,351`, `23,457`). `large/test_8.cnf` is the heavier-tail contrast case: only `62.76%` of successful relocations are in the first two steps (`112,968`, `71,022`, `49,653`, `59,514`), with a noticeably larger `5+` tail at `20.30%`. Combined with the immediately previous reject, the new boundary is clear: “the first two candidates are common” is true, but still not enough to justify manually spelling them out in Python.
- Decision taken: keep the profiler/test upgrade and leave the production solver unchanged. This is a measurement keep, not a solver-speed keep.
- Next recommended step: if the learnt large-clause path is revisited, target a broader structural simplification than another scan-head micro-unroll, and continue checking any such branch specifically against `large/test_8.cnf`, where the deeper successful-relocation tail is materially larger.

## 2026-03-20T16:11:47+01:00 - Rejected root pure-literal shrink-ratio gate

- Objective: revisit the current root pure-literal gate with a stronger classifier that piggybacks on the same iterative fixpoint work, rather than adding another whole-formula prescan.
- Hypothesis: requiring both `>= 2` root pure assignments and a minimum removed-clause ratio might keep the real pure-hit wins while filtering out low-yield search-path perturbation cases better than assignment count alone.
- Files changed: `agent_log.md`, `experiments.jsonl`, `benchmark_summary.md`, `next_steps.md`
- Tests executed: reread `autonomous_sat_agent_prompt.md`, `agent_log.md`, `benchmark_summary.md`, and `next_steps.md`; recomputed per-case root pure assignment counts and clause-removal fractions across the 59-case corpus; built temporary `/tmp/satsolver_rootpure_ratio.py` that counts removed normalized clauses inside the same root-pure fixpoint and gates the presolve with `SATSOLVER_ROOT_PURE_MIN_REMOVAL_RATIO`; swept thresholds `0.00`, `0.05`, `0.08`, `0.10`, and `0.15` on the exact-CLI pure-hit slice; swept `0.00`, `0.05`, `0.08`, and `0.10` on the same-day exact-CLI mixed slice of pure-hit cases plus `large/test_6.cnf`, `special/hard.cnf`, `medium/test_4.cnf`, and `large/test_8.cnf`; ran a forward/reverse exact-CLI A/B for `0.00` vs `0.08`; ran a full repeat-aware exact-CLI 59-case suite on `/tmp/satsolver_rootpure_ratio.py` with `SATSOLVER_ROOT_PURE_MIN_REMOVAL_RATIO=0.08`; `python -m unittest discover -s tests -v` on the retained production solver; SAT and UNSAT checker spot checks on `small/test_1.cnf` and `special/tseitin.cnf`.
- Benchmark results: the slice story looked promising but the suite gate failed decisively. On the exact-CLI pure-hit slice, the best threshold was `0.10`, improving `0.5957s -> 0.5361s`, while `0.08` also beat the local `0.00` baseline at `0.5573s`. On the mixed exact-CLI slice, `0.08` was best, improving `26.0581s -> 25.4692s`; the stricter forward/reverse A/B also stayed positive overall, `25.2493s -> 25.0454s` forward and `25.8285s -> 25.3225s` reverse, driven mostly by `large/test_6.cnf` (`13.4786s -> 13.0595s` forward, `14.5786s -> 13.8249s` reverse) while `special/hard.cnf` regressed in both orders. But the real repeat-aware exact-CLI 59-case suite on the `0.08` gate still lost badly at `31.4387s` representative, `62.8773s` measured, and `63.0960s` wall clock with `59/59` correct, which is far worse than both the retained recent reruns (`27.7868s`, `27.6136s`) and the historical best `27.1974s`.
- Decision taken: discard the shrink-ratio gate and leave the production solver unchanged. The useful result is the new boundary: even a stronger root-pure classifier that piggybacks on the same fixpoint can still win the pure-hit and mixed slices yet fail badly on the real exact-CLI suite.
- Next recommended step: if root pure-literal gating is revisited again, do not rely on assignment count plus removed-clause ratio alone. Future work there needs a materially better full-suite classifier, otherwise effort should move back to the watched-clause propagation and conflict-analysis hotspots.

## 2026-03-20T16:21:10+01:00 - Rejected active-unassigned branch picker

- Objective: test whether `pick_branch_literal()` can be sped up structurally by scanning only the currently unassigned variables instead of all variables on every branch choice.
- Hypothesis: maintaining an active prefix of unassigned variables, while preserving the existing smallest-variable tie break among equal activities, might reduce branch-picker cost enough to matter without changing the search path.
- Files changed: `agent_log.md`, `experiments.jsonl`, `benchmark_summary.md`, `next_steps.md`
- Tests executed: reread `autonomous_sat_agent_prompt.md`, `next_steps.md`, `benchmark_summary.md`, and `agent_log.md`; refreshed `cProfile` on `large/test_6.cnf`; reran `python tools/profile_solver.py large/test_6.cnf special/hard.cnf medium/test_4.cnf large/test_8.cnf`; built then removed temporary `scratch/exp_active_unassigned_branch.py`; `python -m py_compile scratch/exp_active_unassigned_branch.py`; `python -u scratch/exp_active_unassigned_branch.py`; `pkill -f 'python -u scratch/exp_active_unassigned_branch.py'`; `python -m unittest discover -s tests -v`; SAT/UNSAT checker spot checks on `small/test_1.cnf` and `special/tseitin.cnf` using the retained production solver.
- Benchmark results: the branch failed immediately on the first dense hotspot gate, so it never earned a wider run. On `large/test_6.cnf`, the scratch baseline took `15.0435s` with `72,886` decisions and `59,201` conflicts, while the active-unassigned branch took `28.8075s` with `134,042` decisions and `108,066` conflicts. So this was not a clean same-search overhead experiment at all; the mutable unassigned-set maintenance perturbed the search path hard while also making the case much slower.
- Decision taken: discard the active-unassigned branch-picker rewrite and leave the production solver unchanged. The useful result is another boundary on branching work: mutable active-set maintenance is not automatically a structural win just because `pick_branch_literal()` shows up in `cProfile`.
- Next recommended step: if branching is revisited again, demand a much stronger invariance story than “track the unassigned set explicitly,” and keep prioritizing watched-clause propagation and conflict-analysis work ahead of branch-picker state maintenance.

## 2026-03-20T16:28:32+01:00 - Kept branch-frontier profiler counters

- Objective: explain why branch-picker rewrites keep failing by measuring the actual decision frontier shape on the main hotspot families rather than relying on `cProfile` alone.
- Hypothesis: if branch choice is dominated by wide best-activity ties or frequent zero-activity fallbacks, then future `pick_branch_literal()` work should focus on tie handling or zero-activity cases; if the top activity is almost always unique, then mutable active-set schemes are probably adding risk without attacking the real branching cost.
- Files changed: `tools/profile_solver.py`, `tests/test_profile_solver.py`, `agent_log.md`, `experiments.jsonl`, `benchmark_summary.md`, `next_steps.md`
- Tests executed: reread `autonomous_sat_agent_prompt.md`, `agent_log.md`, `benchmark_summary.md`, `next_steps.md`, and the current `satsolver.py` / `tools/profile_solver.py` hot paths; `python -m py_compile tools/profile_solver.py tests/test_profile_solver.py`; `python -m unittest discover -s tests -v`; `python tools/profile_solver.py large/test_6.cnf special/hard.cnf medium/test_4.cnf large/test_8.cnf`.
- Benchmark results: the new profiler says the branch frontier is still wide, but the winner is almost never tied. On `large/test_6.cnf`, branch selection averaged `237.71` unassigned variables per decision, yet the best-activity tie share was only `9 / 72,886` decisions (`0.01%`) and zero-activity choices were `0`. `special/hard.cnf` averaged `166.50` unassigned variables with `13 / 54,245` multiway ties (`0.02%`) and `0` zero-activity choices. `medium/test_4.cnf` averaged `161.67` unassigned variables with `15 / 13,510` multiway ties (`0.11%`) and `0` zero-activity choices. `large/test_8.cnf` averaged `247.72` unassigned variables with `15 / 21,526` multiway ties (`0.07%`) and `0` zero-activity choices. So the branch-picker cost is mostly repeated wide-frontier scanning with an effectively unique winner, not a large amount of tie handling or zero-activity fallback logic.
- Decision taken: keep the profiler/test upgrade and leave the production solver unchanged. This is a measurement keep, not a solver-speed keep.
- Next recommended step: if branching is revisited, do not target best-activity tie handling or zero-activity fallback as the main issue. Any future branching idea needs a read-only scan reduction or stronger invariance story than mutable active-set maintenance, while watched-clause propagation and conflict analysis still remain higher priority overall.

## 2026-03-20T21:27:01+01:00 - Kept ternary satisfied-skip profiler split

- Objective: refine the remaining original-ternary propagation question by measuring how often a triggered watched clause exits immediately because the other watched literal is already true.
- Hypothesis: if problem-ternary trigger events are mostly satisfied skips, then future ternary work should focus on that early exit; if satisfied skips are only a minority, then the real remaining cost is deeper relocation/unit/conflict work and branch reordering around the satisfied-skip exit is lower priority.
- Files changed: `tools/profile_solver.py`, `tests/test_profile_solver.py`, `agent_log.md`, `experiments.jsonl`, `benchmark_summary.md`, `next_steps.md`
- Tests executed: reread `autonomous_sat_agent_prompt.md`, `agent_log.md`, `benchmark_summary.md`, and `next_steps.md`; extended `tools/profile_solver.py` with clause-family satisfied-skip counters plus CLI reporting; added regression coverage in `tests/test_profile_solver.py`; `python -m py_compile tools/profile_solver.py tests/test_profile_solver.py`; `python -m unittest discover -s tests -v`; `python tools/profile_solver.py large/test_6.cnf special/hard.cnf medium/test_4.cnf large/test_8.cnf`.
- Benchmark results: on the main hotspot cases, problem-ternary satisfied skips are a clear minority of trigger events. `large/test_6.cnf` lands at `1,832,487 / (1,832,487 + 7,596,700) = 19.43%`. `special/hard.cnf` lands at `26.36%`, `medium/test_4.cnf` at `24.59%`, and `large/test_8.cnf` at `24.10%`. Learnt ternary clauses are more skip-heavy but still not overwhelmingly so, at `36.47%`, `44.74%`, `48.44%`, and `41.01%` respectively. So the dominant original-ternary cost is still the deeper relocation/unit/conflict path, not the immediate satisfied-skip exit.
- Decision taken: keep the profiler/test upgrade and leave the production solver unchanged. This is a measurement keep, not a solver-speed keep.
- Next recommended step: future original-ternary propagation work should target the relocation/unit/conflict path that follows a non-satisfied trigger, not just early-exit branch ordering around `other_value == TRUE`.

## 2026-03-20T21:32:50+01:00 - Kept ternary unit/conflict family split

- Objective: refine the original-ternary hotspot again by measuring how the non-satisfied trigger path breaks down between relocation, unit propagation, and outright conflict for problem clauses versus learnt clauses.
- Hypothesis: if non-satisfied original-ternary traffic is overwhelmingly relocation-only, then future work should mostly target the replacement path; if units are also a large share, then the next useful solver idea should consider both relocation and unit handling, while conflicts can stay deprioritized if they remain tiny.
- Files changed: `tools/profile_solver.py`, `tests/test_profile_solver.py`, `agent_log.md`, `experiments.jsonl`, `benchmark_summary.md`, `next_steps.md`
- Tests executed: reread `autonomous_sat_agent_prompt.md`, `agent_log.md`, `benchmark_summary.md`, and `next_steps.md`; extended `tools/profile_solver.py` with problem-vs-learnt ternary unit/conflict counters plus CLI reporting; added regression coverage in `tests/test_profile_solver.py`; `python -m py_compile tools/profile_solver.py tests/test_profile_solver.py`; `python -m unittest discover -s tests -v`; `python tools/profile_solver.py large/test_6.cnf special/hard.cnf medium/test_4.cnf large/test_8.cnf`.
- Benchmark results: on the main hotspot cases, non-satisfied original-ternary traffic is a two-way relocation/unit story, not a conflict-heavy one. `large/test_6.cnf` splits problem ternary visits into `61.01%` relocation, `38.31%` unit, and only `0.68%` conflict. `special/hard.cnf` lands at `53.91% / 45.21% / 0.88%`, `medium/test_4.cnf` at `56.42% / 42.67% / 0.90%`, and `large/test_8.cnf` at `51.34% / 47.92% / 0.75%`. Learnt ternary visits show a similar shape (`47.54%..58.10%` relocation, `41.26%..51.73%` unit, `0.64%..0.83%` conflict). So future original-ternary work should target both relocation and unit-heavy paths, while conflict-specific branching remains lower priority.
- Decision taken: keep the profiler/test upgrade and leave the production solver unchanged. This is a measurement keep, not a solver-speed keep.
- Next recommended step: if original-ternary propagation is revisited, prefer ideas that simplify the shared non-satisfied path or reduce both relocation and unit work together, not branches that mainly optimize the rare ternary-conflict tail.

## 2026-03-20T21:45:58+01:00 - Rejected destructured ternary locals in `propagate()`

- Objective: test whether the shared ternary relocation/unit hot path can be sped up structurally by manually unpacking the 3-literal clause into locals and writing the watched order back explicitly, instead of repeatedly indexing the `lits` list inside `propagate()`.
- Hypothesis: because the remaining hotspot is mostly original-clause ternary relocation plus unit work, a tighter ternary path with fewer repeated list-index operations might cut Python overhead without changing traversal order, search counts, or correctness.
- Files changed: `agent_log.md`, `experiments.jsonl`, `benchmark_summary.md`, `next_steps.md`
- Tests executed: reread `autonomous_sat_agent_prompt.md`, `agent_log.md`, `benchmark_summary.md`, and `next_steps.md`; refreshed `cProfile` on `large/test_6.cnf`; built then removed temporary `scratch/exp_ternary_destructure.py`; `python -m py_compile scratch/exp_ternary_destructure.py`; `PYTHONPATH=. python scratch/exp_ternary_destructure.py`; `python -m unittest discover -s tests -v`; `python satsolver.py small/test_1.cnf /tmp/cycle_sat.txt`; `python tools/checker.py small/test_1.cnf /tmp/cycle_sat.txt`; `python satsolver.py special/tseitin.cnf /tmp/cycle_unsat.txt`; `python tools/checker.py special/tseitin.cnf /tmp/cycle_unsat.txt --bruteforce-var-limit 0`; parsed `experiments.jsonl`.
- Benchmark results: the branch was a clean same-search regression and did not earn an exact-CLI follow-up. Forward order regressed from `29.6744s` to `31.0203s`, with identical conflict counts on all five cases and the biggest losses on `large/test_6.cnf` (`13.3523s -> 13.8948s`) and `special/hard.cnf` (`8.8790s -> 9.4342s`). Reverse order regressed from `28.7830s` to `30.9007s`, again with identical conflict counts on every case, including `large/test_8.cnf` (`16,430` conflicts both ways) and `large/test_6.cnf` (`59,201` conflicts both ways). So this was pure Python overhead, not a search-path improvement.
- Decision taken: discard the destructured-ternary branch and leave the production solver unchanged. Manual local unpacking plus write-back of ternary watched literals is not enough, by itself, to beat the retained baseline on the hotspot slice.
- Next recommended step: keep looking for a larger structural simplification of the shared original-ternary relocation/unit path. Future propagation work should not assume that reducing a few `lits[...]` reads inside the ternary branch is already a meaningful end-to-end win.

## 2026-03-20T21:57:47+01:00 - Rejected normalized-clause reuse through root presolve and ingest

- Objective: test whether startup work can be reduced by normalizing each clause once and reusing that normalized form for both root pure-literal discovery and problem-clause ingest, instead of normalizing once in `find_iterative_root_pure_literals()` and then again in `add_problem_clause()`.
- Hypothesis: because the current serial path duplicates clause normalization on every solve, reusing normalized clauses end-to-end might preserve the same search path while shaving enough front-loaded work to help the exact CLI suite even if hotspot search counts stay the same.
- Files changed: `agent_log.md`, `experiments.jsonl`, `benchmark_summary.md`, `next_steps.md`
- Tests executed: reread `autonomous_sat_agent_prompt.md`, `agent_log.md`, `benchmark_summary.md`, and `next_steps.md`; refreshed `rg` on normalization call sites in `satsolver.py`; refreshed `cProfile` on `small/test_1.cnf`; built then removed temporary `scratch/exp_reuse_normalized_clauses.py`; `python -m py_compile scratch/exp_reuse_normalized_clauses.py`; `PYTHONPATH=. python scratch/exp_reuse_normalized_clauses.py --benchmark`; exact-CLI full-suite baseline on `satsolver.py` via `python benchmark_suite.py satsolver /tmp/baseline_cli_cycle.txt small medium large special satlib_subset satlib_more --bruteforce-var-limit 16 --cli-script satsolver.py`; fixed the scratch CLI import path; exact-CLI full-suite candidate rerun via `python benchmark_suite.py satsolver /tmp/candidate_cli_cycle.txt small medium large special satlib_subset satlib_more --bruteforce-var-limit 16 --cli-script scratch/exp_reuse_normalized_clauses.py`; `python -m unittest discover -s tests -v`; `python satsolver.py small/test_1.cnf /tmp/cycle_sat2.txt`; `python tools/checker.py small/test_1.cnf /tmp/cycle_sat2.txt`; `python satsolver.py special/tseitin.cnf /tmp/cycle_unsat2.txt`; `python tools/checker.py special/tseitin.cnf /tmp/cycle_unsat2.txt --bruteforce-var-limit 0`.
- Benchmark results: the branch preserved exact hotspot search counts but did not clear the real suite gate. On `small/test_1.cnf`, `cProfile` showed the duplicated work clearly, with `52` calls to `normalize_clause_literals()` and `26` calls to `add_problem_clause()` for a `26`-clause instance. On the five-case serial hotspot slice, the candidate was essentially a same-search tie: forward improved from `28.4287s` to `28.1825s`, while reverse regressed from `28.2020s` to `28.2754s`, with identical conflict counts on every case in both orders. The exact required CLI full suite then decided it: the retained baseline solved `59/59` in `29.7802s`, while the normalized-reuse candidate also solved `59/59` but regressed slightly to `29.8429s`. So removing the duplicate normalization pass was not enough to produce a real submission-path win.
- Decision taken: discard the normalized-clause reuse branch and leave the production solver unchanged. This is another same-search startup cleanup that looked plausible locally but still failed the stricter exact-CLI suite bar.
- Next recommended step: if preprocessing/startup cleanup is revisited, demand a broader suite-level signal than “one less normalization pass” before touching production. The more promising path still looks like a larger propagation or conflict-analysis simplification rather than another front-loaded helper cleanup.

## 2026-03-20T22:07:22+01:00 - Kept trail-append and large-family profiler splits

- Objective: explain the remaining `append()` / `pop()` pressure from `cProfile` by separating propagation trail growth, decision trail growth, learnt-literal construction in `analyze()`, and learnt-large watched-clause traffic instead of treating all list churn as one bucket.
- Hypothesis: if the hot list churn is mostly branch decisions or learnt-clause construction, then future work should move toward branch picking or `analyze()` list building; if propagation trail growth and watch relocation still dominate, then the next real win remains in the shared watched-clause relocation/unit path, especially on original ternary clauses.
- Files changed: `tools/profile_solver.py`, `tests/test_profile_solver.py`, `agent_log.md`, `experiments.jsonl`, `benchmark_summary.md`, `next_steps.md`
- Tests executed: reread `autonomous_sat_agent_prompt.md`, `agent_log.md`, `benchmark_summary.md`, `next_steps.md`, and the current `cProfile` output for `large/test_6.cnf`; extended `tools/profile_solver.py` with decision-vs-propagation trail-append counters, large-clause problem-vs-learnt relocation/unit/conflict splits, and `analyze()` learnt-literal append counters plus CLI reporting; tightened decomposition invariants in `tests/test_profile_solver.py`; `python -m py_compile tools/profile_solver.py tests/test_profile_solver.py`; `python -m unittest discover -s tests -v`; `python tools/profile_solver.py large/test_6.cnf special/hard.cnf medium/test_4.cnf large/test_8.cnf`.
- Benchmark results: the new counters make the remaining list churn shape much clearer. On `large/test_6.cnf`, propagation trail appends were `3,230,374`, `analyze()` learnt-literal appends were only `787,921`, and decision trail appends were `72,886`; within propagation trail growth, ternary units contributed `3,037,579` appends (`94.03%`), while large-clause units contributed only `191,005`. `special/hard.cnf` showed the same shape at `2,191,117` propagation trail appends versus `571,151` learnt-literal appends, with ternary units contributing `91.48%` of propagation trail growth. `medium/test_4.cnf` and `large/test_8.cnf` landed at `94.49%` and `95.92%` ternary-unit share respectively. The watch side is even stronger: watch relocations were still larger than propagation trail appends on all four hotspots, from `826,585` vs `505,286` on `medium/test_4.cnf` up to `6,556,559` vs `3,230,374` on `large/test_6.cnf`. So the visible `append()` / `pop()` cost is still mainly watched-clause relocation churn plus ternary-unit trail growth, not decision enqueues or learnt-literal construction. The new large-family split also says all active large-clause units/conflicts/relocations on these dense pure-3-SAT hotspots are learnt-clause traffic, not original-problem clauses.
- Decision taken: keep the profiler/test upgrade and leave the production solver unchanged. This is a measurement keep, not a solver-speed keep.
- Next recommended step: future propagation work should keep targeting the shared relocation/unit path before the current unit handoff, with watch relocation churn and original-ternary unit growth as the main list-operation targets. `analyze()` list building and decision trail growth are now clearly secondary.

So the raw `pop()` hotspot is now explained much more directly: it is overwhelmingly watcher-list churn from relocations plus deleted-learnt cleanup, not decision-level stack maintenance.
- Decision taken: keep the profiler/test upgrade and leave the production solver unchanged. This is a measurement keep, not a solver-speed keep.
- Next recommended step: if the next solver experiment targets list churn, it should attack watched-clause relocation/removal work directly. Trail-limit stack pops are now clearly too small to matter relative to watcher churn.

PY` to extract batch-mix shares; parsed `experiments.jsonl`.
